## DOC-002807 — project/research/projection_simplex_h3_selected_gate_20261003/PROOF.md

来源：D:\Danus\runtime\projects\gd-exact-profile-20260926\research\projection_simplex_h3_selected_gate_20261003\PROOF.md

原始字节数：36973；原始 SHA-256：8a929fd731b47a486a08118fe1a1bbcc020ace3b400f9543b0b4f4565d002fb7

证据标签：source_document_no_new_verification。以下内容原样收录，不因汇编而获得新的证明地位。

<!-- BEGIN_SOURCE_BODY DOC-002807 -->
The sole declared predecessor is 1252b8823bb5a290, used only for its
smooth-convex interpolation necessity; Part A also derives the scaled
inequality directly. Every new claim in (A)-(C) is proved below, and no
unverified report or audit is a mathematical premise. Symbols and equation
numbers have the local scopes explicitly specified in each proof part.

## Proof Part A: finite-word projection-simplex universality

Fix the finite word h from (A). In this part S=sum_(i<n)h_i, p is its number of positive entries, and W_n^simplex(h) denotes the restricted supremum in (A). The local number of distinct nonzero gradients is m_A; it is unrelated to the enclosing schedule parameter m of Parts B and C. The local lift scale is R_lift. All numbered references in a part refer to that same part unless another part is explicitly named.

## 2. Elementary projection facts and the complete minimum set

For a nonempty compact convex set `D`, the maximizer defining `F_D` is unique:
completing the square identifies it with the unique nearest point `P_D(q)`.
For `v in D`, the exact characterization is

    P_D(q)=v  iff  <q-v,w-v><=0 for every w in D.     (2.1)

Necessity follows by differentiating the squared distance on the segment
from `v` to `w` at zero. For sufficiency, expand

    ||q-w||^2 = ||q-v||^2 + ||w-v||^2
                -2<q-v,w-v> >= ||q-v||^2.

For a finite convex hull it is enough to check every generating point,
because the left side of (2.1) is affine in `w`.

The two normal inequalities for `v=P_D(q)` and `v'=P_D(q')` imply

    <q-q',v-v'> >= ||v-v'||^2.

Thus the projection is 1-Lipschitz. Evaluating the affine maxima at each
other's optimizers gives

    0 <= F_D(q')-F_D(q)-<P_D(q),q'-q>
      <= <P_D(q')-P_D(q),q'-q> <= ||q'-q||^2.

Consequently `F_D` is differentiable with gradient `P_D` and is globally
1-smooth. It is convex as a maximum of affine functions of `q` and finite
because `D` is compact.

When `0 in D`, the minimum is zero and its COMPLETE minimum set is the cone

    C_D = { a : <a,v><=0 for every v in D }.          (2.2)

Membership makes every defining expression nonpositive, while `v=0` gives
zero. Conversely, if `<a,v>>0` for some `v in D`, then `theta v in D` for
`0<=theta<=1`, and

    <a,theta v>-||theta v||^2/2 > 0

for sufficiently small positive `theta`. Thus `a` is not a minimizer.
The polar here always means the cone with threshold ZERO, not a body polar
with threshold one. It is nonempty, closed, and convex, so every point has
a closest point in it. Existence follows, for example, by restricting a
distance-minimizing sequence to a bounded ball and taking a convergent
subsequence.

## 3. Strict smoothness supplies the necessary strict rows

Let `0<ell<1`, let `f` be convex differentiable and ell-smooth, and translate
and subtract its chosen optimum so that `f(0)=min f=0`. Use its ACTUAL GD
samples `i=0,...,n`, including the terminal sample, with

    x_{i+1}=x_i-h_i g_i,
    g_i=grad f(x_i),  f_i=f(x_i).

Add a reference label `*` with `x_*=g_*=f_*=0`. Define

    Q_ji = f_j-f_i+<g_j,x_i-x_j>+||g_j-g_i||^2/2,
    gamma = (1-ell)/(2ell)>0.

The ell-smooth interpolation necessity is

    Q_ji <= -gamma ||g_j-g_i||^2.                   (3.1)

This is the smoothness-scaled necessity part of accepted local fact
`1252b8823bb5a290`; none of its duality or attainment conclusions is used.
One can also derive the precise orientation directly: the convex function
`k(x)=f(x)-<g_j,x>` has a minimum at `x_j`, and an ell-smooth descent step
from `x_i` of length `1/ell` gives

    k(x_j) <= k(x_i-(g_i-g_j)/ell)
             <= k(x_i)-||g_i-g_j||^2/(2ell).

Rearrange and add the `||g_i-g_j||^2/2` term to obtain (3.1).

Put

    eta_i = <x_i,g_i>-f_i-||g_i||^2/2,
    H_i = sum_{t<i} h_t eta_t,
    eta_* = 0.

The optimum row `Q_{i,*}=-eta_i` gives

    eta_i >= gamma ||g_i||^2 >= 0,                 (3.2)

and `Q_{*,i}=-f_i+||g_i||^2/2` gives

    f_i-||g_i||^2/2 >= gamma ||g_i||^2.             (3.3)

In particular every nonzero gradient has STRICTLY positive `eta_i`.
If `g_i=0`, convexity makes `x_i` a minimizer, so `f_i=eta_i=0`.
All later gradients and positions then remain zero-gradient/stationary.

If `g_i=g_j=g`, the two elementary convexity inequalities force

    f_i-f_j=<g,x_i-x_j>.

Therefore `eta_i=eta_j`, and direct substitution gives `Q_ji=0`.
The same conclusion applies to equal zero gradients and to the reference
label. Distinct positions with the same gradient need not be identified as
positions; only their gradient/height vertex labels will be identified.

## 4. exact sparse normal-guard construction

For `R_lift>0`, define

    v_i=(g_i,eta_i/R_lift),  v_*=0,
    D_R=conv{0,v_0,...,v_n},
    z_i=(x_i,-R_lift-H_i/R_lift),
    a_R=(0,-R_lift).

This is a compact finite polytope in one extra coordinate, with at most
`n+2` generating points. The point `a_R` belongs to its full minimum cone
because `<a_R,v_i>=-eta_i<=0`. For `z_0=(x_0,-R_lift)`,

    ||z_0-a_R||=||x_0||.                           (4.1)

The claimed identity is exact. For every physical sample `i` and every
`j in {*,0,...,n}`,

    <z_i-v_i,v_j-v_i>
      = <x_i-g_i,g_j-g_i>-(eta_j-eta_i)
        -(H_i+eta_i)(eta_j-eta_i)/R_lift^2
      = Q_ji-(H_i+eta_i)(eta_j-eta_i)/R_lift^2.          (4.2)

For clarity, the middle terms expand to

    <x_i-g_i,g_j-g_i>-(eta_j-eta_i)
      = f_j-f_i+<g_j,x_i-x_j>+||g_j-g_i||^2/2.

This checks both the orientation `Q_ji` and the sign of the vertical
correction. No position associated with the reference `*` appears in the
normal test other than through the zero jet in `Q_*i`.

If `g_i=g_j`, both terms in (4.2) vanish. Otherwise `-Q_ji>0` by (3.1).
Define the data-dependent finite number

    A = max_{i=0,...,n; j in {*,0,...,n}; g_i!=g_j}
          (H_i+eta_i)(eta_i-eta_j)_+ / (-Q_ji),      (4.3)

with maximum over an empty set equal to zero. Finiteness follows from
finitely many finite data and strictly positive denominators. Here
`H_i+eta_i>=0` uses the nonnegative steps.

For `R_lift^2>=A`, all expressions in (4.2) are nonpositive. By (2.1),

    P_{D_R}(z_i)=v_i,  for EVERY physical i=0,...,n. (4.4)

The prospective positions satisfy

    z_{i+1}=z_i-h_i v_i,                            (4.5)

because `H_{i+1}=H_i+h_i eta_i`. Equations (4.4)--(4.5), by induction from
the stated initial point, prove that these are the ACTUAL ordinary GD
iterates on the single fixed function `F_{D_R}`. All original horizontal
positions are reproduced exactly. The added coordinate moves by
`-h_i eta_i/R_lift`; it is not held fixed.

The value at every actual sample is also exact:

    F_{D_R}(z_i)
      = <z_i,v_i>-||v_i||^2/2
      = f_i-C_i/R_lift^2,
    C_i = eta_i H_i+eta_i^2/2 >= 0.                (4.6)

The optimum/reference row is included in the guard; it is not left to a
heuristic value estimate. If `g_i!=0`, (4.4) already implies positive value
via `F_{D_R}(z_i)>=||v_i||^2/2>0`. Taking `R_lift` larger also makes the explicit
correction in (4.6) as small as desired.

This proves the sparse normal-guard construction. If only `R_lift^2=A` is used, some
distinct-vertex rows may be equalities; that boundary is insufficient for
the generic perturbation proposal. The next section deliberately chooses
strict quantitative slack instead.

## 5. Quantitative strict margins, including zero-gradient samples

Suppose at least one sampled gradient is nonzero. List the distinct nonzero
gradient values as `g^1,...,g^{m_A}`, with their well-defined heights
`eta^1,...,eta^{m_A}`. Write

    g^0=0,  eta^0=0,
    a(i) in {0,...,m_A},
    g_i=g^{a(i)},  eta_i=eta^{a(i)}.

The equality of heights proved above makes this label map unambiguous,
including repeated gradients at different physical times. Let

    delta = min_{0<=a<b<=m_A} ||g^a-g^b|| > 0.        (5.1)

Choose

    R_lift^2 >= max(1,2A).                              (5.2)

For a physical `i` and a target label `b`, define

    N_{ib}=<z_i-v^{a(i)},v^b-v^{a(i)}>,
    v^b=(g^b,eta^b/R_lift).

When `a(i)!=b`, choose any original representative of `b` (or the reference
label for zero) in (4.2). The possible positive correction is at most
`A(-Q_ji)/R_lift^2<=(-Q_ji)/2`. Thus

    N_{ib} <= Q_ji/2
            <= -(gamma/2)||g_i-g^b||^2
            <= -gamma delta^2/2.                  (5.3)

For equal labels it is identically zero. This retains every PHYSICAL
sample row, even when one vertex label occurs repeatedly; those samples
have different `H_i` and must not be silently collapsed as time indices.

If `g_i=0` and `b!=0`, the exact formula is even more transparent:

    N_{ib}=Q_{ji}-H_i eta^b/R_lift^2<0.

So zero-gradient samples supply strict inequalities against every nonzero
vertex. Their equality against zero itself is a harmless identical-vertex
row, not a missing strictness condition.

The strict margins also show that each distinct nonzero `v^b` is an exposed
vertex of `D_R`: at an occurrence of label `b`, the vector `z_i-v^b`
strictly exposes it among all other generating points. Zero is exposed by
`a_R`, since all `eta^b>0`. Thus, under (5.2), the sparse body already has
exactly `m_A+1` vertices, although they need not be affinely independent.

## 6. an explicit simplex perturbation with exact dynamics

Append `m_A` new mutually orthogonal coordinates. Let `e_1,...,e_(m_A)` be their
standard basis and set `e_0=0`. For a number `epsilon>0`, define

    w^b=(v^b,epsilon e_b),  0<=b<=m_A,
    D_{R_lift,epsilon}=conv{0,w^1,...,w^{m_A}}.

In particular `w^0=0`; a zero-gradient vertex is NOT given a new nonzero
component. Every occurrence of the same nonzero gradient receives exactly
the same component. Define cumulative chronological vectors

    b_i=sum_{t<i} h_t e_{a(t)},
    Z_i=(z_i,-epsilon b_i),
    A_R=(a_R,0).

The INITIAL appended coordinates are zero. Their subsequent values above
are recomputed from all the actual preceding steps, rather than assumed to
stay zero. By construction,

    Z_0=(x_0,-R_lift,0),
    Z_{i+1}=Z_i-h_i w^{a(i)}.                       (6.1)

The normal condition has an explicit perturbation identity. With
`a=a(i)`, for every target label `b`,

    <Z_i-w^a,w^b-w^a>
       = N_{ib}
         -epsilon^2 <b_i+e_a,e_b-e_a>.             (6.2)

There is no term linear in `epsilon`, because old and new coordinate blocks
are orthogonal and the new initial block is zero.

Let `b_{i,a}=<b_i,e_a>` for `a>0`, and take `b_{i,0}=0`. All these numbers
are nonnegative and at most `S`. The coefficient of `epsilon^2` in (6.2)
is exactly

    0                         if a=b;
    b_{i,a}-b_{i,b}+1          if a,b>0 and a!=b;
    b_{i,a}+1                  if a>0 and b=0;
    -b_{i,b}                   if a=0 and b>0.

Hence it is at most `S+1`. Equal-label rows remain exactly zero for every
physical occurrence. At zero-gradient samples the perturbation only makes
the distinct-vertex normal inequality more negative.

Choose, in addition to (5.2),

    0<epsilon^2 <= gamma delta^2/[4(S+1)].          (6.3)

Combining (5.3) and (6.2) gives, for all different labels,

    <Z_i-w^{a(i)},w^b-w^{a(i)}>
         <= -gamma delta^2/4 < 0.                 (6.4)

The identical-label rows are zero. Thus every generator satisfies the normal
condition, and (2.1) proves

    P_{D_{R_lift,epsilon}}(Z_i)=w^{a(i)}

at every physical index, including the terminal. Along with (6.1), this
proves that `Z_i` is the ACTUAL GD run of `F_{D_{R_lift,epsilon}}` under the same
word. This is a quantitative proof of the proposal, not an appeal to
genericity or continuity at a supposedly strict equality.

The vectors `w^1,...,w^{m_A}` are linearly independent: projection of a linear
relation to the new coordinate block gives `epsilon` times every coefficient
equal to zero. Therefore this body is a genuine `m_A`-simplex in its span,
with exactly `m_A+1` vertices including zero.

The nearby minimum remains exact:

    <A_R,w^b>=-eta^b<0 for b>0,
    ||Z_0-A_R||=||x_0||.                           (6.5)

Thus the whole minimum cone has not been replaced by the origin, and no
large artificial coordinate is paid for in the initial distance.

Finally, every perturbed value has the explicit formula

    F_{D_{R_lift,epsilon}}(Z_i)
      = f_i-C_i/R_lift^2-epsilon^2 beta_i,
    beta_i=<b_i,e_{a(i)}>+||e_{a(i)}||^2/2.         (6.6)

If `a(i)=0`, both `C_i` and `beta_i` are zero and the value is exactly zero.
Otherwise

    beta_i=b_{i,a(i)}+1/2,
    0<beta_i<=S+1/2.

This proves value continuity quantitatively while preserving the original
strict-run horizontal trajectory exactly before compression.

For any desired strict-run terminal value loss `tau>0`, the simultaneous
explicit choices

    R_lift^2 >= max(1,2A,2C_n/tau),
    0<epsilon^2 <= min{
        gamma delta^2/[4(S+1)],
        tau/[2(S+1/2)]
    }                                              (6.7)

make the total loss in (6.6) at most `tau`, while all different-label normal
guards remain strictly negative. Every bound is finite and strictly
positive where a positive perturbation is needed. No uniform lower bound
on `delta`, or uniform upper bound on `A`, is assumed.

## 7. Compression to exactly m_A dimensions and the own closest radius

The construction so far lives in the original space plus `1+m_A` coordinates.
Let

    E=span{w^1,...,w^{m_A}},  dim E=m_A,

and let `P_E` be orthogonal projection onto `E`. Since the entire body lies
in `E`, for every `Z` and every `w in D_{R_lift,epsilon}`,

    <Z,w>=<P_E Z,w>,
    ||Z-w||^2=||P_E Z-w||^2+||Z-P_E Z||^2.

Consequently both the value and the selected projection gradient are
preserved:

    F_D(Z)=F_D(P_E Z),
    P_D(Z)=P_D(P_E Z).

Put `q_i=P_E Z_i` and `a=P_E A_R`. Then

    q_{i+1}=q_i-h_i w^{a(i)},
    P_D(q_i)=w^{a(i)},
    F_D(q_i)=F_D(Z_i),
    <a,w^b>=-eta^b<=0.

Thus the compressed points are an actual GD run of the same simplex
function regarded as a function on the `m_A`-dimensional Euclidean space
`E`; choosing an orthonormal basis identifies it with `R^(m_A)`. The point `a`
is a true minimizer, and

    ||q_0-a||=||P_E(Z_0-A_R)||<=||x_0||.            (7.1)

This handles the selected radius. The OWN closest radius is preserved
exactly under the compression as well. Writing `C_E` for the polar cone
inside `E`, the ambient minimum set is

    C_D = C_E + E^perp.

Orthogonal decomposition therefore gives

    dist(Z_0,C_D)=dist(P_E Z_0,C_E).                 (7.2)

If the terminal value is positive, neither starting point is a minimizer:
a zero gradient would leave every following iterate fixed with zero value.
The shared radius in (7.2), denoted `rho`, is then positive, and (7.1) gives

    0<rho<=||x_0||.

For `r=||x_0||>0`, if `tau<f_n`, (6.7) consequently yields

    2 F_D(q_n)/rho^2 >= 2(f_n-tau)/r^2.             (7.3)

The positivity of the lower numerator is explicit; no inequality with a
negative numerator is reversed by changing the denominator. Neither the
selected compressed radius nor the closest radius is asserted to equal the
original `r`. The inequality is sufficient.

The number of distinct sampled positions is at most `p+1`, because a zero
step leaves the position unchanged and each positive step can add at most
one position. Therefore

    m_A <= p+1,
    number of simplex vertices = m_A+1 <= p+2.

This count includes the terminal gradient. Repeated gradients can reduce
`m_A` further, even when their positions differ. It is not legitimate to
remove a distinct terminal gradient merely because it is unqueried by the
last update; it remains part of this value/normal construction.

## 8. Regularization of an arbitrary 1-smooth original run

Return now to an arbitrary convex differentiable 1-smooth `f`, with chosen
minimum zero at zero and initial radius `r>0`. Its original samples are
`x_i,g_i,f_i`. For `0<c<1`, define `f_c=c f` and run the SAME word on it,
starting from the same `x_0`. Denote the new actual positions by `x_i^c`.
Its smoothness bound is `ell=c<1`, and its minimum set is unchanged.

Set

    M=max_{0<=i<=n}||g_i||,
    Pi_i=product_{t<i}(1+h_t),
    B_i=(1-c)M(Pi_i-1).

Subtract the two actual updates and use 1-Lipschitz continuity of `grad f`:

    ||x_{i+1}^c-x_{i+1}||
      <= (1+c h_i)||x_i^c-x_i||+(1-c)h_i||g_i||
      <= (1+h_i)||x_i^c-x_i||+(1-c)h_i M.

Induction gives

    ||x_i^c-x_i||<=B_i.                             (8.1)

Convexity and smoothness at the ORIGINAL terminal point imply

    |f(x_n^c)-f_n|<=||g_n||B_n+B_n^2/2.

Therefore the terminal error after scaling the objective is explicitly

    |f_c(x_n^c)-f_n| <= e_c,
    e_c=(1-c)f_n+c||g_n||B_n+c B_n^2/2 -> 0.        (8.2)

For example, with `K=f_n+M^2(Pi_n-1)+M^2(Pi_n-1)^2/2`, one has
`e_c<=(1-c)K`. For a positive original terminal value, `K>0`, so a finite
choice of `c<1` sufficiently close to one is immediate from this bound.

The exact horizontal reproduction in Sections 4--6 is of the ACTUAL
`f_c` run. There is no assertion that this regularized run is horizontally
identical to the original `f` run.

Given any positive original score and `eta>0`, choose

    0<d<min(f_n,eta r^2/2).

First choose `c<1` so that `e_c<d/2`; the new strict-run terminal value is
positive. Then choose a strict-run loss `0<tau<d/2`, and choose `R_lift,epsilon`
by (6.7) for THAT strict run. Its total value loss relative to the original
is less than `d`, so the resulting simplex run has positive terminal value
and own-closest score

    2 F_D(q_n)/rho^2
      >= 2(f_n-d)/r^2
      > 2f_n/r^2-eta.                              (8.3)

This sequence of choices is important: `c` first, then its finite actual
transcript, then `R_lift`, then a positive `epsilon`, then span compression.
The parameters may depend on the instance, the entire fixed word, and the
tolerance. The number of distinct gradient labels may also depend on `c`,
but it always satisfies the same bound `m_A<=p+1`.

## 9. Equality of suprema, scale L, and boundary audit

Every simplex function in assertion (A) is a legal convex differentiable 1-smooth
function with a minimizer, by Section 2. Thus

    W_n^simplex(h)<=W_n(h).

For every original positive-score instance and every tolerance, (8.3)
produces an admissible simplex instance with no more than `p+2` vertices
and dimension no more than `p+1`. Taking suprema gives the reverse
inequality and proves assertion (A).

Original zero-score runs do not require a ratio with zero closest radius.
The restricted class is nonempty and has nonnegative scores. It even has
a positive score for every fixed word: in dimension one take `D=[0,1]`
and start at `q_0=1+S`. Every iterate stays at least one, all projection
gradients equal one, and the terminal value is `1/2`, with closest radius
`1+S`. Its score is `1/(1+S)^2>0`.

For arbitrary `L>0`, replace the original function by

    tilde f(x)=[f(x_*+x)-f(x_*)]/L.

The normalized step is `h_i`, exactly corresponding to the original
`h_i/L` step. Multiply the final simplex function by `L`. Its gradient
is `L P_D`, its algorithm again uses the same word, and its normalized
score is still `2F_D(q_n)/rho^2`.

The complete boundary audit is as follows.

1. **Empty word and all-zero words.** Here `p=0`, positions do not move,
   there is at most one nonzero gradient label, and `H_i=b_i=0`. A positive
   score uses a one-dimensional segment simplex. Equations (4.6) and (6.6)
   reduce respectively to losses `eta_i^2/(2R_lift^2)` and `epsilon^2/2`.
   The regularization error (8.2) still applies with `Pi_n=1`.
2. **Zero steps among positive steps.** Duplicate positions and gradients
   use the same label. Both cumulative vectors simply omit their zero
   contributions, and no strictness between identical vertices is required.
3. **Repeated nonzero gradients at different times.** Convexity forces
   equal heights. Use one vertex and one appended coordinate for the whole
   label, but retain every physical normal row and its actual `H_i,b_i`.
   Equation (6.2) is identically zero for a same-label comparison regardless
   of accumulated time. Different-label comparisons have the explicit bound.
4. **Zero-gradient samples.** Their function values and heights are zero,
   and their vertex remains exactly zero. Against nonzero vertices their
   old rows are strict and their new correction is nonpositive. From such
   a sample onward the original and lifted runs are stationary. If the
   terminal score is positive, no zero-gradient sample can have occurred.
5. **All gradients zero.** The run has score zero. The exact sparse lift
   is the point body `{0}`. A zero-dimensional simplex convention can
   describe it, but no own-closest quotient is formed and this case is
   unnecessary for the positive-score supremum argument.
6. **Origin/optimum rows.** The reference zero vertex is included in `A`
   and in every perturbed normal test. Its perturbation coefficient at a
   nonzero sample is `b_{i,a}+1`, the potentially positive term controlled
   by `S+1`. It is not dropped. The chosen nearby minimizer is checked
   separately by its negative inner products with all nonzero vertices.
7. **Degenerate sparse bodies.** Degeneracy before the orthogonal
   perturbation causes no issue. Positive `epsilon` makes all nonzero
   vertices independent. Compression then gives precisely their span,
   without a full-dimensionality assumption on the original gradient data.
8. **Huge finite steps and small strict margins.** `A`, `S`, and `Pi_n` may
   be huge, while `gamma` and `delta` may be tiny. For a fixed finite run
   they remain finite, with `gamma,delta>0`, so (6.7) permits a finite `R_lift`
   and a strictly positive `epsilon`. No word-uniform conditioning estimate
   is asserted, and no limit is interchanged with a supremum or infimum.
9. **Closest-minimum distance.** Positivity is obtained before dividing;
   compression preserves the own closest radius exactly by (7.2), while
   the explicitly chosen minimum gives its upper bound by the original
   radius. No claim of convergence of these radii is needed.

The scope remains nonnegative finite steps and finite horizons. Negative
steps are not covered by the signs used in `H_i`, `b_i`, and the estimates.



## Proof Part B: the same-m actual H3 U identity

## 1. Fixed parameters, bodies, and notation

Fix ONE integer m>=1536, throughout all children. Define

    epsilon=1/(4m), beta=1-epsilon, lambda=1+sqrt(2),
    c=lambda(1+epsilon), a=beta/c,
    H_0=(), H_(j+1)=(H_j,1+a*c^j,H_j),
    S_j=sum H_j, C_j=1+S_j.

The physical H2 is (A,B,A), where A=1+a and B=1+beta. Write

    s=S2=2A+B, C=1+s, k=a*c^2=beta*c,
    h=C+k, S=S3=s+(1+k)+s=2C+k-1.

Thus h is the MASS of the prefix H2 followed by its root bridge, not an
extra physical step. The seven physical steps are
(A,B,A,1+k,A,B,A). Neither m nor the leaf parameters reset.

For one fixed nonempty compact convex set D0 in a finite-dimensional real
Euclidean space, containing0, put

    P(v)=argmin_(g in D0)||v-g||^2,
    F(v)=max_(g in D0)(<v,g>-||g||^2/2),
    U_d(v)=max_(g in D0)(2<v,g>-d||g||^2), d>0.

A physical word uses x_(i+1)=x_i-h_i g_i and g_i=P(x_i).
All auxiliary projections in this proof also use this SAME D0.
No approximation to the physical trajectory is used.

The accepted interpolation orientation is fact1252b8823bb5a290:

    Q_ij=f_i-f_j+<g_i,x_j-x_i>+||g_i-g_j||^2/2 <=0.       (1.1)

Only the interpolation necessity of predecessor 1252b8823bb5a290 is
used. All composition and H2 identities needed here are proved in this
part. For clarity, the scalar s here is S2; the constant sqrt(2)-1 is
denoted u. The primitive coefficient R_prim below is unrelated to the
lift scale R_lift of Part A or the objective normalization R_n.

## 2. Elementary projection facts and exact original rows

Projection optimality is

    <v-P(v),g-P(v)> <=0 for all g in D0.                 (2.1)

Necessity follows by minimizing squared distance on each segment from
P(v) to g. Sufficiency follows by expanding ||v-g||^2. The minimizer is
unique by strict convexity of squared distance. Adding the two conditions
for inputs v,w gives ||P(v)-P(w)||^2<=<v-w,P(v)-P(w)>; hence P is
1-Lipschitz. Completion of the square identifies P(v) as the unique
maximizer in F. The two maximizing inequalities bound the first-order
remainder of F by ||t||||P(v+t)-P(v)||, proving grad F=P. Thus F is
convex, differentiable and1-smooth.

Its minimum is0. The minimizers are exactly

    K0={z:<z,g><=0 for every g in D0}.

If z is in K0, all candidates in F(z) are nonpositive and g=0 attains0.
If some <z,g> is positive, sufficiently small positive multiples of g
belong to D0 and give a positive value. For every z_star in K0 and M>0,

    U_M(x)<=||x-z_star||^2/M,                           (2.2)

by completing the square in2<x-z_star,g>-M||g||^2 and using
<z_star,g><=0. No origin-radius replacement is made.

At physical or auxiliary samples of this same F, direct substitution of
f_i=<x_i,g_i>-||g_i||^2/2 into (1.1) gives

    Q_ij=<x_j-g_j,g_i-g_j>.                            (2.3)

Every row below is therefore an actual same-body projection normal.
Auxiliary samples introduce no physical gradient-descent step.

For a word b of mass t, input v, endpoint z, define its primitive energy

    V_b=2sum_(i<N)b_i(f_i-f_N)
        +sum_(i<N)b_i(1-b_i)||g_i||^2-t(t+1)||g_N||^2.

Telescoping ||x_i||^2-||x_(i+1)||^2 proves

    V_b=||v||^2-||z+tP(z)||^2.                         (2.4)

Indeed the value and nonterminal gradient terms sum to
||v||^2-||z||^2-2t f_N; substitution of f_N gives (2.4).

## 3. H2 and off-root identities retained jointly

For an actual step x_v=x_u-l g_u, the two ordered rows are

 Q_uv=f_u-f_v+(1/2-l)||g_u||^2-<g_u,g_v>+||g_v||^2/2,
 Q_vu=f_v-f_u+||g_u||^2/2+(l-1)<g_u,g_v>+||g_v||^2/2.

Coefficient collection gives

 V_(l)=-2Q_uv-2(1+l)Q_vu+(2-l^2)||g_u-g_v||^2,          (3.1)
 ||g_u||^2-||g_v||^2
   =(2/l-1)||g_u-g_v||^2-(2/l)(Q_uv+Q_vu), l>0.         (3.2)

These identities keep both formal gradients.

For general finite nonnegative left and right words, define their capacities
P_L=1+sum(left) and P_R=1+sum(right). Insert the
actual bridge1+t, 0<=t<P_R. Let p be its query and z the parent endpoint;
j ranges over the nonterminal right queries with steps b_j. Define

 K=(2P_L+P_R+t)/(P_R-t),
 Delta=P_L P_R-t(P_L+P_R+t),
 R_prim=(P_L+t)Delta/(P_R-t),
 E=sum_j b_j Q_(j,p)+Q_(z,p)+t Q_(p,z)+sum_j b_j Q_(j,z).

On the actual history, without any primitive assumption, one has

    V_parent=V_left+K V_right-(K-1)E
                         +R_prim(||g_p||^2-||g_z||^2).       (3.3)

Here is a coefficient check of the off-root term. Expansion of E gives
value coefficients

    2sum_j b_j f_j+(t-P_R)f_p+(2-P_R-t)f_z

and gradient terms

 sum_j b_j(1-b_j)||g_j||^2
 +[(P_R-t)/2-t^2]||g_p||^2+(P_R+t)||g_z||^2/2.

All mixed terms cancel: with G=sum b_j g_j, the two sums contribute
respectively t<G,g_p>+||G||^2/2 and-||G||^2/2-<G,g_z>, in addition to
half their displayed diagonals; Q_(z,p) contributes t<g_z,g_p>+<G,g_z>,
and tQ_(p,z) contributes-t<g_p,G>-t<g_p,g_z>. The remaining value
coefficients match because(K-1)(P_R-t)=2(P_L+t). The unmatched gradient
coefficients are P_L^2-Kt^2=R_prim and K P_R^2-(P_L+P_R+t)^2=-R_prim. Factoring
after multiplication by P_R-t verifies these two identities. This proves
(3.3) without an exact-root substitution or a changed trajectory.

For H2 only, put C1=1+A=2+a and

 K1=(3C1+beta)/(C1-beta),
 Delta1=C1^2-beta(2C1+beta), R_prim1=(C1+beta)Delta1/(C1-beta),
 deltaA=2-A^2.

Let u=sqrt(2)-1. Then a=u(1-epsilon)/(1+epsilon), 2u+u^2=1, and

 u C1-beta=epsilon(1+epsilon-2u^2)/(1+epsilon)>0,
 Delta1=(u C1-beta)(lambda C1+beta)>0.                (3.4)

Also1<A<sqrt(2),1<B<2,C1>beta, and K1>1. Apply (3.3) to the two
leaves, then (3.2) to bridge B and the final leaf A. Together with (3.1)
this gives the ENTIRE H2 identity, on its four original gradients,

 V_H2=-sum_(i!=j)Lambda_ij Q_ij
       +e0||g0-g1||^2+e1||g1-g2||^2+e2||g2-g3||^2,   (3.5)

 e0=deltaA, e1=R_prim1(2/B-1)=R_prim1 epsilon/B,
 e2=K1 deltaA+R_prim1(2/A-1),

 Lambda_01=2,
 Lambda_10=2(1+A),
 Lambda_12=2R_prim1/B,
 Lambda_21=(K1-1)A+2R_prim1/B,
 Lambda_13=(K1-1)beta,
 Lambda_31=K1-1,
 Lambda_23=2K1+(K1-1)A+2R_prim1/A,
 Lambda_32=2K1(1+A)+2R_prim1/A.                           (3.6)

All other multipliers are zero; none is negative. The value coefficients
are(2A,2B,2A,-2s): for the bridge they give
-2A+(K1-1)(C1-beta)=2B, and at the last nonterminal point they give
-2(K1-1)A+2K1 A=2A. The square remainder is a positive weighted path
Laplacian on all four gradients. No formal coordinates are identified.

There is also the conservative bound

    e_i>=epsilon^2/2, i=0,1,2.                       (3.7)

To check it, deltaA=[2u epsilon/(1+epsilon)](sqrt(2)+A)>epsilon/2;
u>1/4, epsilon<1, and sqrt(2)+A>2 suffice. Formula (3.4), u<1/2,
and C1>2 give uC1-beta>epsilon/4 and lambda C1+beta>4. Hence
Delta1>epsilon and R_prim1>epsilon. Since B<2, (3.7) follows. These estimates follow from the displayed identities.

For the actual H3 root use C=1+s and k=a*c^2. Equation (3.3) reads

 K2=(3C+k)/(C-k), Delta2=C^2-k(2C+k),
 R_prim2=(C+k)Delta2/(C-k),
 V_H3=V_L+K2 V_R-(K2-1)E2+R_prim2(||g3||^2-||g7||^2).     (3.8)

E2 is the displayed E with p=3,z=7 and j=4,5,6. Its physical right
steps are A,B,A. Every row and the two H2 histories remain actual.

The sign is genuinely R_prim2<0. Indeed
C=5-epsilon+2u(1-epsilon)/(1+epsilon), k=lambda(1-epsilon^2),
and lambda=5u+2u^2 give

 k-uC=epsilon[u+4u^2/(1+epsilon)-lambda epsilon]>0.

Here u>2/5, lambda<5/2 and epsilon<1/8 ensure the last bracket is
positive. Thus Delta2=(uC-k)(lambda C+k)<0. Primitive positivity is
not a premise of the H3 proof.

## 4. Every actual maximization remainder

For any actual word of mass t, input v, endpoint z and trailing d>=1,
write

 M=d+2t, q=P(z/d), p=P(v/M),
 bbar=[v-z+(d+t)q]/M,
 Rem(v,M,bbar)=M||p-bbar||^2+2<v-Mp,p-bbar>.           (4.1)

The point bbar belongs to D0: its physical gradient weights are the
actual nonnegative steps, and its q weight is d+t; their sum is M.
Each term in Rem is nonnegative by (2.1). Comparing the maximum at p
with the feasible point bbar, then expanding the squares, gives EXACTLY

 U_M(v)-U_d(z)
   =[||v||^2-||z+tq||^2]/M+Rem(v,M,bbar).             (4.2)

For instance v-M bbar=z-(d+t)q, which makes the expansion immediate:
the numerator after subtracting M U_d(z) is
||v||^2-||z||^2-2t<z,q>-t^2||q||^2.

Put g=P(z). Comparing the optimizers of U_d(z) at two different d values
gives, for1<=d1<d2 and q_i=P(z/d_i),

 d1(||q1||^2-||q2||^2)
 <=2<z,q1-q2><=d2(||q1||^2-||q2||^2).

It follows that the squared-norm and inner-product differences are
nonnegative. Consequently the exact radial term

 Lrad(t,z,d)=2t<z,g-q>+t^2(||g||^2-||q||^2)>=0         (4.3)

satisfies

 U_M(v)-U_d(z)=[V_word+Lrad(t,z,d)]/M+Rem(v,M,bbar).  (4.4)

This proves the H2 U law from (3.5), for ALL starts and d>=1. It also
retains the actual remainders, instead of assigning them values from a
different trajectory.

All remainders are themselves sums of original augmented normal rows
and one square. In (4.1), let bbar=sum theta_i g_i with theta_i>=0 and
sum theta_i=1, including the actual auxiliary q sample. The initial
auxiliary sample has input v/M and gradient p. Formula (2.3) gives

 Rem=M||p-bbar||^2-2M sum_i theta_i Q_(i,p).           (4.5)

Here Q_(i,p)=<v/M-p,g_i-p>. Thus no separate unproved positivity
principle is concealed in the notation Rem.

## 5. the primitive-left / actual-auxiliary bridge identity

Keep a general left word of mass s_L and
capacity C_L=1+s_L, with actual input x and endpoint v. Let a_p=P(v).
Apply the ACTUAL bridge1+k, k>=0, to obtain

 w=v-(1+k)a_p, h=C_L+k=s_L+1+k.

Let D>0 be an independent trailing parameter, and define

 M=D+2h, r=P(w/D), p0=P(x/M),
 zP=[x-w+(D+h)r]/M,
 Np=<v-a_p,a_p-r>, Nr=<w-D r,r-a_p>,
 Theta=h[C_L D-k(C_L+D+k)]/(D+k).                  (5.1)

Both Np and Nr are nonnegative. In augmented row notation,
Np=-Q_(r,p), Nr=-D Q_(p,r), where the r sample has input w/D and the
p sample is the physical bridge query v. Every point is actual.
The mixture zP uses all left physical gradients, the bridge gradient
with weight1+k, and r with weightD+h. These weights sum to M.

Without any assumed signs for the primitive energy V_L, one has

 U_M(x)-U_D(w)
   = [V_L+Theta||a_p-r||^2
        +2h(1+C_L/(D+k))Np+2h C_L/(D+k)Nr]/M
       +Rem(x,M,zP).                                (5.2)

Here is a full derivation. Put X=v+s_L a_p and e=a_p-r. Then

 w+h r=X-h e,
 ||x||^2-||w+h r||^2=V_L+2h<X,e>-h^2||e||^2.        (5.3)

The actual projection normals give the exact relations

 Np+Nr=(D+k)<a_p,e>-D||e||^2,
 <X,e>=C_L<a_p,e>+Np
       =C_L D||e||^2/(D+k)
          +(1+C_L/(D+k))Np+C_L Nr/(D+k).            (5.4)

Substitute (5.4) in (5.3) and then apply (4.2) to the prefix mass h
with trailing D. The square coefficient is
h[2C_L D/(D+k)-h]=Theta, proving (5.2).

Therefore, if

    V_L>=0 and C_L D>=k(C_L+D+k),                   (5.5)

then U_D(w)<=U_(D+2h)(x). This is a reusable quantified lemma with
independent C_L,D and actual physical bridge k. The numerical capacity
condition is not a substitute for the displayed actual left reserve.
A right word of mass s_R may be appended if its actual U law holds at
trailing d; use D=d+2s_R. No equality C_L=1+s_R is required by (5.2).

## 6. Application to both actual H2 children: nonnegative H3 identity

Now specialize to the actual H3. Write

 x=x0, v=x3, a_p=g3, w=x4, y=x7, b=g7,
 D=d+2s, M=d+2S=D+2h,
 q=P(y/d), r=P(w/D), p0=P(x/M),
 zR=[w-y+(d+s)q]/D,
 zP=[x-w+(D+h)r]/M,
 RR=Rem(w,D,zR), RP=Rem(x,M,zP),
 LR=Lrad(s,y,d).

The initial maximizer of the right child is EXACTLY r, the final
auxiliary maximizer in (5.2). There is no independent choice or reset.
Let V_L and V_R be the actual H2 energies (3.5), using respectively
indices0,1,2,3 and4,5,6,7.

The scalar coefficient is strictly positive for every d>=1. Since
beta>9/10, c<5/2, one has a>1/3, C=4+2a+beta>5, and
k=lambda(1-epsilon^2)<5/2<C/2. Also D>=2C-1. Hence

 C D-k(C+D+k)=(C-k)D-k(C+k)
   > (C/2)(2C-1)-3C^2/4=C(C-2)/4>0.                (6.1)

There is a uniform-in-d lower bound for the actual square coefficient:
D>9C/5 and k<C/2 imply k/(D+k)<5/23, so

 Theta/h=C-k-2Ck/(D+k)>C/2-10C/23=3C/46,
 Theta>3C(C+k)/46.                                 (6.2)

Summing (5.2) and the actual right-child identity (4.4) gives

 U_M(x)-U_d(y)
  = V_L/M+(V_R+LR)/D
    +[Theta||a_p-r||^2
       +2h(1+C/(D+k))Np+2h C/(D+k)Nr]/M
    +RP+RR.                                        (6.3)

Every term on the right is nonnegative after substituting the complete
H2 formula (3.5). Equations (3.5)-(3.6), (4.5), and the explicit two
normal rows after (5.1) make (6.3) a nonnegative original-row identity
on the physical and actual auxiliary samples of one fixed body.
In particular it retains the actual adjacent-gradient reserves

 epsilon^2/(2M) sum_(i=0)^2||g_i-g_(i+1)||^2
 +epsilon^2/(2D) sum_(i=4)^6||g_i-g_(i+1)||^2,

as well as the root auxiliary square of coefficientTheta/M.
No claim is made that the adjacent squares alone pay the root defect.

This proves assertion (B).
At d=1, U1=2F; (2.2) also yields

    2F(T_H3 x)<=dist(x,K0)^2/(1+2S3).

This remains a projection-gradient-class assertion. No arbitrary-height
representation or full-function asymptotic conclusion is being used.



## Proof Part C: full seven-step value and sharpness

For a fixed body D0 containing0, its complete minimum set C_D0 is the polar cone proved above. For any selected minimum x_star in C_D0 and any d>0, every g in D0 satisfies x_star.g<=0. Therefore
 2x.g-d||g||^2 <=2(x-x_star).g-d||g||^2
 <=||x-x_star||^2/d
by completing the square. Taking the maximum gives U_d(x)<=||x-x_star||^2/d. Also U_1=2F_D0. Part B at d=1 now gives
 2F_D0(T_H3 x)<=||x-x_star||^2/(1+2S3).
This holds in particular for every origin-vertex simplex of the dimensions required by Part A. Taking its full restricted supremum and using Part A yields W_7(H_3)<=1/(1+2S3). This uses a fixed finite word for each fixed enclosing m and no interchange of a horizon limit and a supremum.

For the reverse inequality put S=S3 and tau=1/(1+2S). The one-dimensional function f(x)=x^2/2 for |x|<=tau and f(x)=tau*|x|-tau^2/2 outside is globally convex, differentiable,1-smooth and uniquely minimized at0. Start at x0=1. Every partial sum of the nonnegative word lies in[0,S], so induction gives x_i=1-tau*sum_(j<i)h_j>=(1+S)/(1+2S)>=tau. Hence every used gradient equals tau, including the boundary case. At the actual seventh-step terminal,
 2f(x_7)=2tau-(2S+1)tau^2=tau.
The original selected radius is1, giving W_7(H_3)>=tau. Both inequalities prove(C). Multiplying each objective by L and dividing each physical step by L preserves the original W=2R normalization for general advertised L.

This finishes only(A),(B),(C). There is no asserted H_j U bound for all depths, no all-function asymptotic improvement, no uniform all-word minimax lower bound, and no original Goal completion.

<!-- BEGIN_SOURCE_BODY DOC-002808 -->
Projection-simplex universality for finite words and exact full-class value of the actual seven-step H_3 block.

All spaces, norms, and inner products are finite-dimensional real Euclidean ones. For every integer n>=0, every finite predetermined nonnegative word h=(h_0,...,h_(n-1)), and every advertised L>0, define W_n(h) as the supremum of
    2(f(x_n)-f(x_star))/(L||x_0-x_star||^2)
over every convex differentiable function f with gradient Lipschitz constant at most L and an attained minimum, every selected minimizer x_star, and every x_0!=x_star, using the actual updates
    x_(i+1)=x_i-(h_i/L)grad f(x_i), 0<=i<n.
The schedule is fixed before the function and start are chosen. Empty words and zero steps are included. Write R_n(h)=W_n(h)/2, and p=#{i:0<=i<n and h_i>0}.

(A) For each fixed word h, W_n(h) equals the supremum restricted, at normalized smoothness one, to the functions
    F_D(x)=max_(g in D)(<x,g>-||g||^2/2),
    D=conv{0,w_1,...,w_k} in R^k, 1<=k<=p+1,
where w_1,...,w_k are linearly independent. Thus D is a full-dimensional simplex with the origin as a vertex. The restriction retains all starts, all selected minimizers unequal to the start, and the same actual word. Here conv denotes convex hull, P_D is Euclidean projection onto D, grad F_D=P_D, min F_D=0, and the complete minimum set is the polar cone
    C_D={z in R^k:<z,g><=0 for every g in D}.
For positive scores one may equivalently use the closest-minimum denominator dist(x_0,C_D)^2. For general L the restricted objectives are L F_D and the steps are h_i/L, preserving the same score. This is equality of suprema, with no assertion of finite-parameter exact representation or attainment for each source run, or uniform conditioning or approximation over words.

(B) For every integer m>=1536 set
    epsilon=1/(4m), beta=1-epsilon,
    c=(1+sqrt(2))(1+epsilon), a=beta/c,
    H_0=(), H_(j+1)=(H_j,1+a*c^j,H_j), 0<=j<3.
Every child uses this same m and these same parameters. Let S3 be the sum of the seven entries of H_3. For every nonempty compact convex set D0 containing zero in any positive finite dimension, define, for d>0,
    U_d(x)=max_(g in D0)(2<x,g>-d||g||^2).
Let T_H3 be the actual composition of x -> x-h_i P_D0(x) over the seven original steps of H_3 in order. Then for every start x and every real d>=1,
    U_d(T_H3 x)<=U_(d+2S3)(x).
All physical and auxiliary projections use the same set D0. No interior or full-dimensionality assumption on D0 is required.

(C) For every m>=1536 and its actual seven-step H_3 from (B), the full original function class satisfies
    W_7(H_3)=1/(1+2S3).
The lower equality is attained by a one-dimensional Huber function with the original selected-minimum radius. These assertions make no all-depth H_j U claim and do not resolve the original asymptotic minimax problem.

<!-- BEGIN_SOURCE_BODY DOC-002809 -->
# Selected complete candidate: projection-simplex universality and the actual H3 base block

UNVERIFIED, pending the one separately allocated project verifier gate.
The three assertions below are one self-contained selected theorem. None is an asymptotic resolution of the original minimax Goal.

Normalize advertised smoothness to1. For every finite nonnegative word h of length n, let W_n(h) be the supremum of2(f(x_n)-f_star)/||x_0-x_star||^2 over every finite-dimensional convex differentiable function with1-Lipschitz gradient and an attained minimum, every selected minimizer x_star, and every x_0!=x_star, under x_(i+1)=x_i-h_i grad f(x_i). At general advertised L>0 use h_i/L and the original denominator L||x_0-x_star||^2. Thus W=2R in the project's normalization.

(A) Put p=#{i:h_i>0}. For each such fixed finite word, W_n(h) equals its supremum restricted to F_D(x)=max_(g in D)(x.g-||g||^2/2), where D=conv{0,w_1,...,w_k} is a full-dimensional simplex in R^k,1<=k<=p+1, and w_1,...,w_k are linearly independent. Its gradient is P_D and its complete minimum set is the polar cone C_D={z:z.g<=0 for every g in D}. Arbitrary starts and actual selected minimizers are retained; positive scores may equivalently use dist(x_0,C_D). The equality is of suprema, not finite-parameter exact representation or attainment for every source run. No uniform conditioning or approximation bound over words is asserted.

(B) For every integer m>=1536 set eps=1/(4m), beta=1-eps, c=(1+sqrt2)(1+eps), a=beta/c. Define H_0=(), H_(j+1)=(H_j,1+a*c^j,H_j), using this SAME m. Take only its actual seven-step H_3, with mass S3=sum H_3. For every finite-dimensional nonempty compact convex body D0 containing0, let U_d(x)=max_(g in D0)(2x.g-d||g||^2), d>0, and let T_H3 be the actual composition x->x-h P_D0(x) over these seven original steps. Then, for every start x and every d>=1,
 U_d(T_H3 x)<=U_(d+2S3)(x).
All auxiliary projections use the same D0. The bound covers arbitrary bodies and starts, not only a fixed trajectory or origin-interior bodies.

(C) Consequently, for every m>=1536 and the actual seven-step H_3 just defined,
 W_7(H_3)=1/(1+2S3)
over the FULL original function class and true selected-minimum normalization.

Symbols are local to each proof part. In particular the number of distinct gradients called m inside Part A is unrelated to the enclosing schedule parameter m in Parts B/C; the local R lift radius and local R primitive coefficient are also distinct. The canonical glossary must preserve these explicit local scopes.

## Proof Part A: finite-word projection-simplex universality

## 2. Elementary projection facts and the complete minimum set

For a nonempty compact convex set `D`, the maximizer defining `F_D` is unique:
completing the square identifies it with the unique nearest point `P_D(q)`.
For `v in D`, the exact characterization is

    P_D(q)=v  iff  <q-v,w-v><=0 for every w in D.     (2.1)

Necessity follows by differentiating the squared distance on the segment
from `v` to `w` at zero. For sufficiency, expand

    ||q-w||^2 = ||q-v||^2 + ||w-v||^2
                -2<q-v,w-v> >= ||q-v||^2.

For a finite convex hull it is enough to check every generating point,
because the left side of (2.1) is affine in `w`.

The two normal inequalities for `v=P_D(q)` and `v'=P_D(q')` imply

    <q-q',v-v'> >= ||v-v'||^2.

Thus the projection is 1-Lipschitz. Evaluating the affine maxima at each
other's optimizers gives

    0 <= F_D(q')-F_D(q)-<P_D(q),q'-q>
      <= <P_D(q')-P_D(q),q'-q> <= ||q'-q||^2.

Consequently `F_D` is differentiable with gradient `P_D` and is globally
1-smooth. It is convex as a maximum of affine functions of `q` and finite
because `D` is compact.

When `0 in D`, the minimum is zero and its COMPLETE minimum set is the cone

    C_D = { a : <a,v><=0 for every v in D }.          (2.2)

Membership makes every defining expression nonpositive, while `v=0` gives
zero. Conversely, if `<a,v>>0` for some `v in D`, then `theta v in D` for
`0<=theta<=1`, and

    <a,theta v>-||theta v||^2/2 > 0

for sufficiently small positive `theta`. Thus `a` is not a minimizer.
The polar here always means the cone with threshold ZERO, not a body polar
with threshold one. It is nonempty, closed, and convex, so every point has
a closest point in it. Existence follows, for example, by restricting a
distance-minimizing sequence to a bounded ball and taking a convergent
subsequence.

## 3. Strict smoothness supplies the necessary strict rows

Let `0<ell<1`, let `f` be convex differentiable and ell-smooth, and translate
and subtract its chosen optimum so that `f(0)=min f=0`. Use its ACTUAL GD
samples `i=0,...,n`, including the terminal sample, with

    x_{i+1}=x_i-h_i g_i,
    g_i=grad f(x_i),  f_i=f(x_i).

Add a reference label `*` with `x_*=g_*=f_*=0`. Define

    Q_ji = f_j-f_i+<g_j,x_i-x_j>+||g_j-g_i||^2/2,
    gamma = (1-ell)/(2ell)>0.

The ell-smooth interpolation necessity is

    Q_ji <= -gamma ||g_j-g_i||^2.                   (3.1)

This is the smoothness-scaled necessity part of accepted local fact
`1252b8823bb5a290`; none of its duality or attainment conclusions is used.
One can also derive the precise orientation directly: the convex function
`k(x)=f(x)-<g_j,x>` has a minimum at `x_j`, and an ell-smooth descent step
from `x_i` of length `1/ell` gives

    k(x_j) <= k(x_i-(g_i-g_j)/ell)
             <= k(x_i)-||g_i-g_j||^2/(2ell).

Rearrange and add the `||g_i-g_j||^2/2` term to obtain (3.1).

Put

    eta_i = <x_i,g_i>-f_i-||g_i||^2/2,
    H_i = sum_{t<i} h_t eta_t,
    eta_* = 0.

The optimum row `Q_{i,*}=-eta_i` gives

    eta_i >= gamma ||g_i||^2 >= 0,                 (3.2)

and `Q_{*,i}=-f_i+||g_i||^2/2` gives

    f_i-||g_i||^2/2 >= gamma ||g_i||^2.             (3.3)

In particular every nonzero gradient has STRICTLY positive `eta_i`.
If `g_i=0`, convexity makes `x_i` a minimizer, so `f_i=eta_i=0`.
All later gradients and positions then remain zero-gradient/stationary.

If `g_i=g_j=g`, the two elementary convexity inequalities force

    f_i-f_j=<g,x_i-x_j>.

Therefore `eta_i=eta_j`, and direct substitution gives `Q_ji=0`.
The same conclusion applies to equal zero gradients and to the reference
label. Distinct positions with the same gradient need not be identified as
positions; only their gradient/height vertex labels will be identified.

## 4. Mechanism 1: exact sparse normal-guard construction

For `R>0`, define

    v_i=(g_i,eta_i/R),  v_*=0,
    D_R=conv{0,v_0,...,v_n},
    z_i=(x_i,-R-H_i/R),
    a_R=(0,-R).

This is a compact finite polytope in one extra coordinate, with at most
`n+2` generating points. The point `a_R` belongs to its full minimum cone
because `<a_R,v_i>=-eta_i<=0`. For `z_0=(x_0,-R)`,

    ||z_0-a_R||=||x_0||.                           (4.1)

The claimed identity is exact. For every physical sample `i` and every
`j in {*,0,...,n}`,

    <z_i-v_i,v_j-v_i>
      = <x_i-g_i,g_j-g_i>-(eta_j-eta_i)
        -(H_i+eta_i)(eta_j-eta_i)/R^2
      = Q_ji-(H_i+eta_i)(eta_j-eta_i)/R^2.          (4.2)

For clarity, the middle terms expand to

    <x_i-g_i,g_j-g_i>-(eta_j-eta_i)
      = f_j-f_i+<g_j,x_i-x_j>+||g_j-g_i||^2/2.

This checks both the orientation `Q_ji` and the sign of the vertical
correction. No position associated with the reference `*` appears in the
normal test other than through the zero jet in `Q_*i`.

If `g_i=g_j`, both terms in (4.2) vanish. Otherwise `-Q_ji>0` by (3.1).
Define the data-dependent finite number

    A = max_{i=0,...,n; j in {*,0,...,n}; g_i!=g_j}
          (H_i+eta_i)(eta_i-eta_j)_+ / (-Q_ji),      (4.3)

with maximum over an empty set equal to zero. Finiteness follows from
finitely many finite data and strictly positive denominators. Here
`H_i+eta_i>=0` uses the nonnegative steps.

For `R^2>=A`, all expressions in (4.2) are nonpositive. By (2.1),

    P_{D_R}(z_i)=v_i,  for EVERY physical i=0,...,n. (4.4)

The prospective positions satisfy

    z_{i+1}=z_i-h_i v_i,                            (4.5)

because `H_{i+1}=H_i+h_i eta_i`. Equations (4.4)--(4.5), by induction from
the stated initial point, prove that these are the ACTUAL ordinary GD
iterates on the single fixed function `F_{D_R}`. All original horizontal
positions are reproduced exactly. The added coordinate moves by
`-h_i eta_i/R`; it is not held fixed.

The value at every actual sample is also exact:

    F_{D_R}(z_i)
      = <z_i,v_i>-||v_i||^2/2
      = f_i-C_i/R^2,
    C_i = eta_i H_i+eta_i^2/2 >= 0.                (4.6)

The optimum/reference row is included in the guard; it is not left to a
heuristic value estimate. If `g_i!=0`, (4.4) already implies positive value
via `F_{D_R}(z_i)>=||v_i||^2/2>0`. Taking `R` larger also makes the explicit
correction in (4.6) as small as desired.

This completes Mechanism 1 with no gap found. If only `R^2=A` is used, some
distinct-vertex rows may be equalities; that boundary is insufficient for
the generic perturbation proposal. The next section deliberately chooses
strict quantitative slack instead.

## 5. Quantitative strict margins, including zero-gradient samples

Suppose at least one sampled gradient is nonzero. List the distinct nonzero
gradient values as `g^1,...,g^m`, with their well-defined heights
`eta^1,...,eta^m`. Write

    g^0=0,  eta^0=0,
    a(i) in {0,...,m},
    g_i=g^{a(i)},  eta_i=eta^{a(i)}.

The equality of heights proved above makes this label map unambiguous,
including repeated gradients at different physical times. Let

    delta = min_{0<=a<b<=m} ||g^a-g^b|| > 0.        (5.1)

Choose

    R^2 >= max(1,2A).                              (5.2)

For a physical `i` and a target label `b`, define

    N_{ib}=<z_i-v^{a(i)},v^b-v^{a(i)}>,
    v^b=(g^b,eta^b/R).

When `a(i)!=b`, choose any original representative of `b` (or the reference
label for zero) in (4.2). The possible positive correction is at most
`A(-Q_ji)/R^2<=(-Q_ji)/2`. Thus

    N_{ib} <= Q_ji/2
            <= -(gamma/2)||g_i-g^b||^2
            <= -gamma delta^2/2.                  (5.3)

For equal labels it is identically zero. This retains every PHYSICAL
sample row, even when one vertex label occurs repeatedly; those samples
have different `H_i` and must not be silently collapsed as time indices.

If `g_i=0` and `b!=0`, the exact formula is even more transparent:

    N_{ib}=Q_{ji}-H_i eta^b/R^2<0.

So zero-gradient samples supply strict inequalities against every nonzero
vertex. Their equality against zero itself is a harmless identical-vertex
row, not a missing strictness condition.

The strict margins also show that each distinct nonzero `v^b` is an exposed
vertex of `D_R`: at an occurrence of label `b`, the vector `z_i-v^b`
strictly exposes it among all other generating points. Zero is exposed by
`a_R`, since all `eta^b>0`. Thus, under (5.2), the sparse body already has
exactly `m+1` vertices, although they need not be affinely independent.

## 6. Mechanism 2: an explicit simplex perturbation with exact dynamics

Append `m` new mutually orthogonal coordinates. Let `e_1,...,e_m` be their
standard basis and set `e_0=0`. For a number `epsilon>0`, define

    w^b=(v^b,epsilon e_b),  0<=b<=m,
    D_{R,epsilon}=conv{0,w^1,...,w^m}.

In particular `w^0=0`; a zero-gradient vertex is NOT given a new nonzero
component. Every occurrence of the same nonzero gradient receives exactly
the same component. Define cumulative chronological vectors

    b_i=sum_{t<i} h_t e_{a(t)},
    Z_i=(z_i,-epsilon b_i),
    A_R=(a_R,0).

The INITIAL appended coordinates are zero. Their subsequent values above
are recomputed from all the actual preceding steps, rather than assumed to
stay zero. By construction,

    Z_0=(x_0,-R,0),
    Z_{i+1}=Z_i-h_i w^{a(i)}.                       (6.1)

The normal condition has an explicit perturbation identity. With
`a=a(i)`, for every target label `b`,

    <Z_i-w^a,w^b-w^a>
       = N_{ib}
         -epsilon^2 <b_i+e_a,e_b-e_a>.             (6.2)

There is no term linear in `epsilon`, because old and new coordinate blocks
are orthogonal and the new initial block is zero.

Let `b_{i,a}=<b_i,e_a>` for `a>0`, and take `b_{i,0}=0`. All these numbers
are nonnegative and at most `S`. The coefficient of `epsilon^2` in (6.2)
is exactly

    0                         if a=b;
    b_{i,a}-b_{i,b}+1          if a,b>0 and a!=b;
    b_{i,a}+1                  if a>0 and b=0;
    -b_{i,b}                   if a=0 and b>0.

Hence it is at most `S+1`. Equal-label rows remain exactly zero for every
physical occurrence. At zero-gradient samples the perturbation only makes
the distinct-vertex normal inequality more negative.

Choose, in addition to (5.2),

    0<epsilon^2 <= gamma delta^2/[4(S+1)].          (6.3)

Combining (5.3) and (6.2) gives, for all different labels,

    <Z_i-w^{a(i)},w^b-w^{a(i)}>
         <= -gamma delta^2/4 < 0.                 (6.4)

The identical-label rows are zero. Thus every generator satisfies the normal
condition, and (2.1) proves

    P_{D_{R,epsilon}}(Z_i)=w^{a(i)}

at every physical index, including the terminal. Along with (6.1), this
proves that `Z_i` is the ACTUAL GD run of `F_{D_{R,epsilon}}` under the same
word. This is a quantitative proof of the proposal, not an appeal to
genericity or continuity at a supposedly strict equality.

The vectors `w^1,...,w^m` are linearly independent: projection of a linear
relation to the new coordinate block gives `epsilon` times every coefficient
equal to zero. Therefore this body is a genuine `m`-simplex in its span,
with exactly `m+1` vertices including zero.

The nearby minimum remains exact:

    <A_R,w^b>=-eta^b<0 for b>0,
    ||Z_0-A_R||=||x_0||.                           (6.5)

Thus the whole minimum cone has not been replaced by the origin, and no
large artificial coordinate is paid for in the initial distance.

Finally, every perturbed value has the explicit formula

    F_{D_{R,epsilon}}(Z_i)
      = f_i-C_i/R^2-epsilon^2 beta_i,
    beta_i=<b_i,e_{a(i)}>+||e_{a(i)}||^2/2.         (6.6)

If `a(i)=0`, both `C_i` and `beta_i` are zero and the value is exactly zero.
Otherwise

    beta_i=b_{i,a(i)}+1/2,
    0<beta_i<=S+1/2.

This proves value continuity quantitatively while preserving the original
strict-run horizontal trajectory exactly before compression.

For any desired strict-run terminal value loss `tau>0`, the simultaneous
explicit choices

    R^2 >= max(1,2A,2C_n/tau),
    0<epsilon^2 <= min{
        gamma delta^2/[4(S+1)],
        tau/[2(S+1/2)]
    }                                              (6.7)

make the total loss in (6.6) at most `tau`, while all different-label normal
guards remain strictly negative. Every bound is finite and strictly
positive where a positive perturbation is needed. No uniform lower bound
on `delta`, or uniform upper bound on `A`, is assumed.

## 7. Compression to exactly m dimensions and the own closest radius

The construction so far lives in the original space plus `1+m` coordinates.
Let

    E=span{w^1,...,w^m},  dim E=m,

and let `P_E` be orthogonal projection onto `E`. Since the entire body lies
in `E`, for every `Z` and every `w in D_{R,epsilon}`,

    <Z,w>=<P_E Z,w>,
    ||Z-w||^2=||P_E Z-w||^2+||Z-P_E Z||^2.

Consequently both the value and the selected projection gradient are
preserved:

    F_D(Z)=F_D(P_E Z),
    P_D(Z)=P_D(P_E Z).

Put `q_i=P_E Z_i` and `a=P_E A_R`. Then

    q_{i+1}=q_i-h_i w^{a(i)},
    P_D(q_i)=w^{a(i)},
    F_D(q_i)=F_D(Z_i),
    <a,w^b>=-eta^b<=0.

Thus the compressed points are an actual GD run of the same simplex
function regarded as a function on the `m`-dimensional Euclidean space
`E`; choosing an orthonormal basis identifies it with `R^m`. The point `a`
is a true minimizer, and

    ||q_0-a||=||P_E(Z_0-A_R)||<=||x_0||.            (7.1)

This handles the selected radius. The OWN closest radius is preserved
exactly under the compression as well. Writing `C_E` for the polar cone
inside `E`, the ambient minimum set is

    C_D = C_E + E^perp.

Orthogonal decomposition therefore gives

    dist(Z_0,C_D)=dist(P_E Z_0,C_E).                 (7.2)

If the terminal value is positive, neither starting point is a minimizer:
a zero gradient would leave every following iterate fixed with zero value.
The shared radius in (7.2), denoted `rho`, is then positive, and (7.1) gives

    0<rho<=||x_0||.

For `r=||x_0||>0`, if `tau<f_n`, (6.7) consequently yields

    2 F_D(q_n)/rho^2 >= 2(f_n-tau)/r^2.             (7.3)

The positivity of the lower numerator is explicit; no inequality with a
negative numerator is reversed by changing the denominator. Neither the
selected compressed radius nor the closest radius is asserted to equal the
original `r`. The inequality is sufficient.

The number of distinct sampled positions is at most `p+1`, because a zero
step leaves the position unchanged and each positive step can add at most
one position. Therefore

    m <= p+1,
    number of simplex vertices = m+1 <= p+2.

This count includes the terminal gradient. Repeated gradients can reduce
`m` further, even when their positions differ. It is not legitimate to
remove a distinct terminal gradient merely because it is unqueried by the
last update; it remains part of this value/normal construction.

## 8. Regularization of an arbitrary 1-smooth original run

Return now to an arbitrary convex differentiable 1-smooth `f`, with chosen
minimum zero at zero and initial radius `r>0`. Its original samples are
`x_i,g_i,f_i`. For `0<c<1`, define `f_c=c f` and run the SAME word on it,
starting from the same `x_0`. Denote the new actual positions by `x_i^c`.
Its smoothness bound is `ell=c<1`, and its minimum set is unchanged.

Set

    M=max_{0<=i<=n}||g_i||,
    Pi_i=product_{t<i}(1+h_t),
    B_i=(1-c)M(Pi_i-1).

Subtract the two actual updates and use 1-Lipschitz continuity of `grad f`:

    ||x_{i+1}^c-x_{i+1}||
      <= (1+c h_i)||x_i^c-x_i||+(1-c)h_i||g_i||
      <= (1+h_i)||x_i^c-x_i||+(1-c)h_i M.

Induction gives

    ||x_i^c-x_i||<=B_i.                             (8.1)

Convexity and smoothness at the ORIGINAL terminal point imply

    |f(x_n^c)-f_n|<=||g_n||B_n+B_n^2/2.

Therefore the terminal error after scaling the objective is explicitly

    |f_c(x_n^c)-f_n| <= e_c,
    e_c=(1-c)f_n+c||g_n||B_n+c B_n^2/2 -> 0.        (8.2)

For example, with `K=f_n+M^2(Pi_n-1)+M^2(Pi_n-1)^2/2`, one has
`e_c<=(1-c)K`. For a positive original terminal value, `K>0`, so a finite
choice of `c<1` sufficiently close to one is immediate from this bound.

The exact horizontal reproduction in Sections 4--6 is of the ACTUAL
`f_c` run. There is no assertion that this regularized run is horizontally
identical to the original `f` run.

Given any positive original score and `eta>0`, choose

    0<d<min(f_n,eta r^2/2).

First choose `c<1` so that `e_c<d/2`; the new strict-run terminal value is
positive. Then choose a strict-run loss `0<tau<d/2`, and choose `R,epsilon`
by (6.7) for THAT strict run. Its total value loss relative to the original
is less than `d`, so the resulting simplex run has positive terminal value
and own-closest score

    2 F_D(q_n)/rho^2
      >= 2(f_n-d)/r^2
      > 2f_n/r^2-eta.                              (8.3)

This sequence of choices is important: `c` first, then its finite actual
transcript, then `R`, then a positive `epsilon`, then span compression.
The parameters may depend on the instance, the entire fixed word, and the
tolerance. The number of distinct gradient labels may also depend on `c`,
but it always satisfies the same bound `m<=p+1`.

## 9. Equality of suprema, scale L, and boundary audit

Every simplex function in (1.1) is a legal convex differentiable 1-smooth
function with a minimizer, by Section 2. Thus

    W_n^simplex(h)<=W_n(h).

For every original positive-score instance and every tolerance, (8.3)
produces an admissible simplex instance with no more than `p+2` vertices
and dimension no more than `p+1`. Taking suprema gives the reverse
inequality and proves the candidate identity (1.1).

Original zero-score runs do not require a ratio with zero closest radius.
The restricted class is nonempty and has nonnegative scores. It even has
a positive score for every fixed word: in dimension one take `D=[0,1]`
and start at `q_0=1+S`. Every iterate stays at least one, all projection
gradients equal one, and the terminal value is `1/2`, with closest radius
`1+S`. Its score is `1/(1+S)^2>0`.

For arbitrary `L>0`, replace the original function by

    tilde f(x)=[f(x_*+x)-f(x_*)]/L.

The normalized step is `h_i`, exactly corresponding to the original
`h_i/L` step. Multiply the final simplex function by `L`. Its gradient
is `L P_D`, its algorithm again uses the same word, and its normalized
score is still `2F_D(q_n)/rho^2`.

The complete boundary audit is as follows.

1. **Empty word and all-zero words.** Here `p=0`, positions do not move,
   there is at most one nonzero gradient label, and `H_i=b_i=0`. A positive
   score uses a one-dimensional segment simplex. Equations (4.6) and (6.6)
   reduce respectively to losses `eta_i^2/(2R^2)` and `epsilon^2/2`.
   The regularization error (8.2) still applies with `Pi_n=1`.
2. **Zero steps among positive steps.** Duplicate positions and gradients
   use the same label. Both cumulative vectors simply omit their zero
   contributions, and no strictness between identical vertices is required.
3. **Repeated nonzero gradients at different times.** Convexity forces
   equal heights. Use one vertex and one appended coordinate for the whole
   label, but retain every physical normal row and its actual `H_i,b_i`.
   Equation (6.2) is identically zero for a same-label comparison regardless
   of accumulated time. Different-label comparisons have the explicit bound.
4. **Zero-gradient samples.** Their function values and heights are zero,
   and their vertex remains exactly zero. Against nonzero vertices their
   old rows are strict and their new correction is nonpositive. From such
   a sample onward the original and lifted runs are stationary. If the
   terminal score is positive, no zero-gradient sample can have occurred.
5. **All gradients zero.** The run has score zero. The exact sparse lift
   is the point body `{0}`. A zero-dimensional simplex convention can
   describe it, but no own-closest quotient is formed and this case is
   unnecessary for the positive-score supremum argument.
6. **Origin/optimum rows.** The reference zero vertex is included in `A`
   and in every perturbed normal test. Its perturbation coefficient at a
   nonzero sample is `b_{i,a}+1`, the potentially positive term controlled
   by `S+1`. It is not dropped. The chosen nearby minimizer is checked
   separately by its negative inner products with all nonzero vertices.
7. **Degenerate sparse bodies.** Degeneracy before the orthogonal
   perturbation causes no issue. Positive `epsilon` makes all nonzero
   vertices independent. Compression then gives precisely their span,
   without a full-dimensionality assumption on the original gradient data.
8. **Huge finite steps and small strict margins.** `A`, `S`, and `Pi_n` may
   be huge, while `gamma` and `delta` may be tiny. For a fixed finite run
   they remain finite, with `gamma,delta>0`, so (6.7) permits a finite `R`
   and a strictly positive `epsilon`. No word-uniform conditioning estimate
   is asserted, and no limit is interchanged with a supremum or infimum.
9. **Closest-minimum distance.** Positivity is obtained before dividing;
   compression preserves the own closest radius exactly by (7.2), while
   the explicitly chosen minimum gives its upper bound by the original
   radius. No claim of convergence of these radii is needed.

The scope remains nonnegative finite steps and finite horizons. Negative
steps are not covered by the signs used in `H_i`, `b_i`, and the estimates.



## Proof Part B: the same-m actual H3 U identity

## 1. Fixed parameters, bodies, and accepted scope

Fix ONE integer m>=1536, throughout all children. Define

    epsilon=1/(4m), beta=1-epsilon, lambda=1+sqrt(2),
    c=lambda(1+epsilon), a=beta/c,
    H_0=(), H_(j+1)=(H_j,1+a*c^j,H_j),
    S_j=sum H_j, C_j=1+S_j.

The physical H2 is (A,B,A), where A=1+a and B=1+beta. Write

    s=S2=2A+B, C=1+s, k=a*c^2=beta*c,
    h=C+k, S=S3=s+(1+k)+s=2C+k-1.

Thus h is the MASS of the prefix H2 followed by its root bridge, not an
extra physical step. The seven physical steps are
(A,B,A,1+k,A,B,A). Neither m nor the leaf parameters reset.

For one fixed nonempty compact convex set D0 in a finite-dimensional real
Euclidean space, containing0, put

    P(v)=argmin_(g in D0)||v-g||^2,
    F(v)=max_(g in D0)(<v,g>-||g||^2/2),
    U_d(v)=max_(g in D0)(2<v,g>-d||g||^2), d>0.

A physical word uses x_(i+1)=x_i-h_i g_i and g_i=P(x_i).
All auxiliary projections in this report also use this SAME D0.
No approximation to the physical trajectory is used.

The accepted interpolation orientation is fact1252b8823bb5a290:

    Q_ij=f_i-f_j+<g_i,x_j-x_i>+||g_i-g_j||^2/2 <=0.       (1.1)

Its necessity proof was reread. The complete applicable primitive and
dominant composition definitions in accepted04219554e41d9750, Sections
10-11, were reread. Its exact-root join is not invoked at an off-root
bridge. The identities needed here, including the old H2 draft, are
proved jointly below. Accepted931b9653ce0f7201 still requires a FULL-class
upper before its conditional negative conclusion applies; no such upper
is inferred here. This is the gradient-projection diagnostic, not the
residual-gradient affine-height representation. W=2R remains unchanged.

For clarity, the scalar s here is S2; the constant sqrt(2)-1 is denoted u.
The original problem is the asymptotic unrestricted comparison of W_n^star
with the original ALL-split Zhang--Jiang benchmark, not this finite H3 law.

## 2. Elementary projection facts and exact original rows

Projection optimality is

    <v-P(v),g-P(v)> <=0 for all g in D0.                 (2.1)

Necessity follows by minimizing squared distance on each segment from
P(v) to g. Sufficiency follows by expanding ||v-g||^2. The minimizer is
unique by strict convexity of squared distance. Adding the two conditions
for inputs v,w gives ||P(v)-P(w)||^2<=<v-w,P(v)-P(w)>; hence P is
1-Lipschitz. Completion of the square identifies P(v) as the unique
maximizer in F. The two maximizing inequalities bound the first-order
remainder of F by ||t||||P(v+t)-P(v)||, proving grad F=P. Thus F is
convex, differentiable and1-smooth.

Its minimum is0. The minimizers are exactly

    K0={z:<z,g><=0 for every g in D0}.

If z is in K0, all candidates in F(z) are nonpositive and g=0 attains0.
If some <z,g> is positive, sufficiently small positive multiples of g
belong to D0 and give a positive value. For every z_star in K0 and M>0,

    U_M(x)<=||x-z_star||^2/M,                           (2.2)

by completing the square in2<x-z_star,g>-M||g||^2 and using
<z_star,g><=0. No origin-radius replacement is made.

At physical or auxiliary samples of this same F, direct substitution of
f_i=<x_i,g_i>-||g_i||^2/2 into (1.1) gives

    Q_ij=<x_j-g_j,g_i-g_j>.                            (2.3)

Every row below is therefore an actual same-body projection normal.
Auxiliary samples introduce no physical gradient-descent step.

For a word b of mass t, input v, endpoint z, define its primitive energy

    V_b=2sum_(i<N)b_i(f_i-f_N)
        +sum_(i<N)b_i(1-b_i)||g_i||^2-t(t+1)||g_N||^2.

Telescoping ||x_i||^2-||x_(i+1)||^2 proves

    V_b=||v||^2-||z+tP(z)||^2.                         (2.4)

Indeed the value and nonterminal gradient terms sum to
||v||^2-||z||^2-2t f_N; substitution of f_N gives (2.4).

## 3. H2 and off-root identities retained jointly

For an actual step x_v=x_u-l g_u, the two ordered rows are

 Q_uv=f_u-f_v+(1/2-l)||g_u||^2-<g_u,g_v>+||g_v||^2/2,
 Q_vu=f_v-f_u+||g_u||^2/2+(l-1)<g_u,g_v>+||g_v||^2/2.

Coefficient collection gives

 V_(l)=-2Q_uv-2(1+l)Q_vu+(2-l^2)||g_u-g_v||^2,          (3.1)
 ||g_u||^2-||g_v||^2
   =(2/l-1)||g_u-g_v||^2-(2/l)(Q_uv+Q_vu), l>0.         (3.2)

These identities keep both formal gradients.

For general left and right words with capacities P_L and P_R, insert the
actual bridge1+t, 0<=t<P_R. Let p be its query and z the parent endpoint;
j ranges over the nonterminal right queries with steps b_j. Define

 K=(2P_L+P_R+t)/(P_R-t),
 Delta=P_L P_R-t(P_L+P_R+t),
 R=(P_L+t)Delta/(P_R-t),
 E=sum_j b_j Q_(j,p)+Q_(z,p)+t Q_(p,z)+sum_j b_j Q_(j,z).

On the actual history, without any primitive assumption, one has

    V_parent=V_left+K V_right-(K-1)E
                         +R(||g_p||^2-||g_z||^2).       (3.3)

Here is a coefficient check of the off-root term. Expansion of E gives
value coefficients

    2sum_j b_j f_j+(t-P_R)f_p+(2-P_R-t)f_z

and gradient terms

 sum_j b_j(1-b_j)||g_j||^2
 +[(P_R-t)/2-t^2]||g_p||^2+(P_R+t)||g_z||^2/2.

All mixed terms cancel: with G=sum b_j g_j, the two sums contribute
respectively t<G,g_p>+||G||^2/2 and-||G||^2/2-<G,g_z>, in addition to
half their displayed diagonals; Q_(z,p) contributes t<g_z,g_p>+<G,g_z>,
and tQ_(p,z) contributes-t<g_p,G>-t<g_p,g_z>. The remaining value
coefficients match because(K-1)(P_R-t)=2(P_L+t). The unmatched gradient
coefficients are P_L^2-Kt^2=R and K P_R^2-(P_L+P_R+t)^2=-R. Factoring
after multiplication by P_R-t verifies these two identities. This proves
(3.3) without an exact-root substitution or a changed trajectory.

For H2 only, put C1=1+A=2+a and

 K1=(3C1+beta)/(C1-beta),
 Delta1=C1^2-beta(2C1+beta), R1=(C1+beta)Delta1/(C1-beta),
 deltaA=2-A^2.

Let u=sqrt(2)-1. Then a=u(1-epsilon)/(1+epsilon), 2u+u^2=1, and

 u C1-beta=epsilon(1+epsilon-2u^2)/(1+epsilon)>0,
 Delta1=(u C1-beta)(lambda C1+beta)>0.                (3.4)

Also1<A<sqrt(2),1<B<2,C1>beta, and K1>1. Apply (3.3) to the two
leaves, then (3.2) to bridge B and the final leaf A. Together with (3.1)
this gives the ENTIRE H2 identity, on its four original gradients,

 V_H2=-sum_(i!=j)Lambda_ij Q_ij
       +e0||g0-g1||^2+e1||g1-g2||^2+e2||g2-g3||^2,   (3.5)

 e0=deltaA, e1=R1(2/B-1)=R1 epsilon/B,
 e2=K1 deltaA+R1(2/A-1),

 Lambda_01=2,
 Lambda_10=2(1+A),
 Lambda_12=2R1/B,
 Lambda_21=(K1-1)A+2R1/B,
 Lambda_13=(K1-1)beta,
 Lambda_31=K1-1,
 Lambda_23=2K1+(K1-1)A+2R1/A,
 Lambda_32=2K1(1+A)+2R1/A.                           (3.6)

All other multipliers are zero; none is negative. The value coefficients
are(2A,2B,2A,-2s): for the bridge they give
-2A+(K1-1)(C1-beta)=2B, and at the last nonterminal point they give
-2(K1-1)A+2K1 A=2A. The square remainder is a positive weighted path
Laplacian on all four gradients. No formal coordinates are identified.

There is also the conservative bound

    e_i>=epsilon^2/2, i=0,1,2.                       (3.7)

To check it, deltaA=[2u epsilon/(1+epsilon)](sqrt(2)+A)>epsilon/2;
u>1/4, epsilon<1, and sqrt(2)+A>2 suffice. Formula (3.4), u<1/2,
and C1>2 give uC1-beta>epsilon/4 and lambda C1+beta>4. Hence
Delta1>epsilon and R1>epsilon. Since B<2, (3.7) follows. These facts
are derived here, not assumed from the earlier unverified H2 report.

For the actual H3 root use C=1+s and k=a*c^2. Equation (3.3) reads

 K2=(3C+k)/(C-k), Delta2=C^2-k(2C+k),
 R2=(C+k)Delta2/(C-k),
 V_H3=V_L+K2 V_R-(K2-1)E2+R2(||g3||^2-||g7||^2).     (3.8)

E2 is the displayed E with p=3,z=7 and j=4,5,6. Its physical right
steps are A,B,A. Every row and the two H2 histories remain actual.

The sign is genuinely R2<0. Indeed
C=5-epsilon+2u(1-epsilon)/(1+epsilon), k=lambda(1-epsilon^2),
and lambda=5u+2u^2 give

 k-uC=epsilon[u+4u^2/(1+epsilon)-lambda epsilon]>0.

Here u>2/5, lambda<5/2 and epsilon<1/8 ensure the last bracket is
positive. Thus Delta2=(uC-k)(lambda C+k)<0. Primitive positivity is
not a premise of the H3 proof.

## 4. Every actual maximization remainder

For any actual word of mass t, input v, endpoint z and trailing d>=1,
write

 M=d+2t, q=P(z/d), p=P(v/M),
 bbar=[v-z+(d+t)q]/M,
 Rem(v,M,bbar)=M||p-bbar||^2+2<v-Mp,p-bbar>.           (4.1)

The point bbar belongs to D0: its physical gradient weights are the
actual nonnegative steps, and its q weight is d+t; their sum is M.
Each term in Rem is nonnegative by (2.1). Comparing the maximum at p
with the feasible point bbar, then expanding the squares, gives EXACTLY

 U_M(v)-U_d(z)
   =[||v||^2-||z+tq||^2]/M+Rem(v,M,bbar).             (4.2)

For instance v-M bbar=z-(d+t)q, which makes the expansion immediate:
the numerator after subtracting M U_d(z) is
||v||^2-||z||^2-2t<z,q>-t^2||q||^2.

Put g=P(z). Comparing the optimizers of U_d(z) at two different d values
gives, for1<=d1<d2 and q_i=P(z/d_i),

 d1(||q1||^2-||q2||^2)
 <=2<z,q1-q2><=d2(||q1||^2-||q2||^2).

It follows that the squared-norm and inner-product differences are
nonnegative. Consequently the exact radial term

 Lrad(t,z,d)=2t<z,g-q>+t^2(||g||^2-||q||^2)>=0         (4.3)

satisfies

 U_M(v)-U_d(z)=[V_word+Lrad(t,z,d)]/M+Rem(v,M,bbar).  (4.4)

This proves the H2 U law from (3.5), for ALL starts and d>=1. It also
retains the actual remainders, instead of assigning them values from a
different trajectory.

All remainders are themselves sums of original augmented normal rows
and one square. In (4.1), let bbar=sum theta_i g_i with theta_i>=0 and
sum theta_i=1, including the actual auxiliary q sample. The initial
auxiliary sample has input v/M and gradient p. Formula (2.3) gives

 Rem=M||p-bbar||^2-2M sum_i theta_i Q_(i,p).           (4.5)

Here Q_(i,p)=<v/M-p,g_i-p>. Thus no separate unproved positivity
principle is concealed in the notation Rem.

## 5. Mechanism1: the primitive-left / actual-auxiliary bridge identity

This is the new payment. Keep a GENERAL left word of mass s_L and
capacity C_L=1+s_L, with actual input x and endpoint v. Let a_p=P(v).
Apply the ACTUAL bridge1+k, k>=0, to obtain

 w=v-(1+k)a_p, h=C_L+k=s_L+1+k.

Let D>0 be an independent trailing parameter, and define

 M=D+2h, r=P(w/D), p0=P(x/M),
 zP=[x-w+(D+h)r]/M,
 Np=<v-a_p,a_p-r>, Nr=<w-D r,r-a_p>,
 Theta=h[C_L D-k(C_L+D+k)]/(D+k).                  (5.1)

Both Np and Nr are nonnegative. In augmented row notation,
Np=-Q_(r,p), Nr=-D Q_(p,r), where the r sample has input w/D and the
p sample is the physical bridge query v. Every point is actual.
The mixture zP uses all left physical gradients, the bridge gradient
with weight1+k, and r with weightD+h. These weights sum to M.

Without any assumed signs for the primitive energy V_L, one has

 U_M(x)-U_D(w)
   = [V_L+Theta||a_p-r||^2
        +2h(1+C_L/(D+k))Np+2h C_L/(D+k)Nr]/M
       +Rem(x,M,zP).                                (5.2)

Here is a full derivation. Put X=v+s_L a_p and e=a_p-r. Then

 w+h r=X-h e,
 ||x||^2-||w+h r||^2=V_L+2h<X,e>-h^2||e||^2.        (5.3)

The actual projection normals give the exact relations

 Np+Nr=(D+k)<a_p,e>-D||e||^2,
 <X,e>=C_L<a_p,e>+Np
       =C_L D||e||^2/(D+k)
          +(1+C_L/(D+k))Np+C_L Nr/(D+k).            (5.4)

Substitute (5.4) in (5.3) and then apply (4.2) to the prefix mass h
with trailing D. The square coefficient is
h[2C_L D/(D+k)-h]=Theta, proving (5.2).

Therefore, if

    V_L>=0 and C_L D>=k(C_L+D+k),                   (5.5)

then U_D(w)<=U_(D+2h)(x). This is a reusable quantified lemma with
independent C_L,D and actual physical bridge k. The numerical capacity
condition is not a substitute for the displayed actual left reserve.
A right word of mass s_R may be appended if its actual U law holds at
trailing d; use D=d+2s_R. No equality C_L=1+s_R is required by (5.2).

## 6. Application to both actual H2 children: nonnegative H3 identity

Now specialize to the actual H3. Write

 x=x0, v=x3, a_p=g3, w=x4, y=x7, b=g7,
 D=d+2s, M=d+2S=D+2h,
 q=P(y/d), r=P(w/D), p0=P(x/M),
 zR=[w-y+(d+s)q]/D,
 zP=[x-w+(D+h)r]/M,
 RR=Rem(w,D,zR), RP=Rem(x,M,zP),
 LR=Lrad(s,y,d).

The initial maximizer of the right child is EXACTLY r, the final
auxiliary maximizer in (5.2). There is no independent choice or reset.
Let V_L and V_R be the actual H2 energies (3.5), using respectively
indices0,1,2,3 and4,5,6,7.

The scalar coefficient is strictly positive for every d>=1. Since
beta>9/10, c<5/2, one has a>1/3, C=4+2a+beta>5, and
k=lambda(1-epsilon^2)<5/2<C/2. Also D>=2C-1. Hence

 C D-k(C+D+k)=(C-k)D-k(C+k)
   > (C/2)(2C-1)-3C^2/4=C(C-2)/4>0.                (6.1)

There is a uniform-in-d lower bound for the actual square coefficient:
D>9C/5 and k<C/2 imply k/(D+k)<5/23, so

 Theta/h=C-k-2Ck/(D+k)>C/2-10C/23=3C/46,
 Theta>3C(C+k)/46.                                 (6.2)

Summing (5.2) and the actual right-child identity (4.4) gives

 U_M(x)-U_d(y)
  = V_L/M+(V_R+LR)/D
    +[Theta||a_p-r||^2
       +2h(1+C/(D+k))Np+2h C/(D+k)Nr]/M
    +RP+RR.                                        (6.3)

Every term on the right is nonnegative after substituting the complete
H2 formula (3.5). Equations (3.5)-(3.6), (4.5), and the explicit two
normal rows after (5.1) make (6.3) a nonnegative original-row identity
on the physical and actual auxiliary samples of one fixed body.
In particular it retains the actual adjacent-gradient reserves

 epsilon^2/(2M) sum_(i=0)^2||g_i-g_(i+1)||^2
 +epsilon^2/(2D) sum_(i=4)^6||g_i-g_(i+1)||^2,

as well as the root auxiliary square of coefficientTheta/M.
No claim is made that the adjacent squares alone pay the root defect.

This proves the stated all-body H3 inequality WITHIN THIS JOINT DRAFT.
At d=1, U1=2F; (2.2) also yields

    2F(T_H3 x)<=dist(x,K0)^2/(1+2S3).

This remains a projection-gradient-class assertion. No arbitrary-height
representation or full-function asymptotic conclusion is being used.



## Proof Part C: full seven-step value and sharpness

For a fixed body D0 containing0, its complete minimum set C_D0 is the polar cone proved above. For any selected minimum x_star in C_D0 and any d>0, every g in D0 satisfies x_star.g<=0. Therefore
 2x.g-d||g||^2 <=2(x-x_star).g-d||g||^2
 <=||x-x_star||^2/d
by completing the square. Taking the maximum gives U_d(x)<=||x-x_star||^2/d. Also U_1=2F_D0. Part B at d=1 now gives
 2F_D0(T_H3 x)<=||x-x_star||^2/(1+2S3).
This holds in particular for every origin-vertex simplex of the dimensions required by Part A. Taking its full restricted supremum and using Part A yields W_7(H_3)<=1/(1+2S3). This uses a fixed finite word for each fixed enclosing m and no interchange of a horizon limit and a supremum.

For the reverse inequality put S=S3 and tau=1/(1+2S). The one-dimensional function f(x)=x^2/2 for |x|<=tau and f(x)=tau*|x|-tau^2/2 outside is globally convex, differentiable,1-smooth and uniquely minimized at0. Start at x0=1. Every partial sum of the nonnegative word lies in[0,S], so induction gives x_i=1-tau*sum_(j<i)h_j>=(1+S)/(1+2S)>=tau. Hence every used gradient equals tau, including the boundary case. At the actual seventh-step terminal,
 2f(x_7)=2tau-(2S+1)tau^2=tau.
The original selected radius is1, giving W_7(H_3)>=tau. Both inequalities prove(C). Multiplying each objective by L and dividing each physical step by L preserves the original W=2R normalization for general advertised L.

This finishes only(A),(B),(C). There is no asserted H_j U bound for all depths, no all-function asymptotic improvement, no uniform all-word minimax lower bound, and no original Goal completion.
